What is your occupation and by whom are you employed?

6 
Q.  
Please state your educational background and experience.  
7 
A.

8 
Bachelor of Arts degree. In 1981, I began my career in the energy business at 
9 
Citizens Energy Corporation in Boston, Massachusetts (“Citizens Energy”). I 
10 
became involved in the natural gas business of Citizens Energy in 1983. Between 
11 
1983 and 1989, I served as Manager, Vice President, President and Chairman of 
12 
Citizens Gas Supply Corporation (a subsidiary of Citizens Energy).  
13 
I started and ran an energy consulting firm, Landmark Associates, from 1989 to 
14 
1993, during which time I consulted on numerous pipeline open access matters, a 
15 
number of Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (“FERC”) Order No. 636 rate 
16 
cases, pipeline certificate cases, fuel supply and gas transportation issues for 
17 
independent power generation projects, international arbitration cases involving 
18 
renegotiation of pipeline gas supply contracts, and natural gas market information 
19 
requirements cases (FERC Order Nos. 587 et seq.). In 1993, I founded 
20 
TransCapacity LP, a software and natural gas information services company.

5 
II.   
Purpose of Testimony and Recommendations  
6 
Q. 
What is the purpose of your testimony?  
7 
A. 
The purpose of my testimony is to provide recommendations regarding how the 
8 
gas supply planning process of The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National 
9 
Grid NY (“KEDNY”) and KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
10 
(“KEDLI”) (collectively, “Companies”) could be improved.    
11 
Q.  
Please provide a summary of your testimony and recommendations.   
12 
A. 
My testimony first details the changed market conditions and new developments 
13 
that compel the need for revisions to the Companies’ gas supply planning process. 
14 
I then provide an overview of the current gas supply planning process in New 
15 
York and compare this process to other states. To improve the Companies’ gas 
16 
supply planning process, I propose three recommendations. I first suggest that the 
17 
Companies should be subject to an annual gas supply process, with discovery 
18 
rights for intervenors and technical conferences as needed. I next suggest that the 
19 
Companies should be required to submit a long-range plan in a format, which 
20 
would become the basis for evaluating future cost recovery. The long-range plan 
21 
would set forth projections of demand and resources to meet that demand, 
22 


--- Page 6 ---
 
 
19-G-0309  
 
 
Direct Testimony of Gregory M. Lander 
19-G-0310                                                       
5 
 
identifying the “all-in” cost for each resource. Finally, I suggest additional 
1 
categories of information the Companies should submit in order to assist the 
2 
Commission in its review of gas supply issues. Taken together, I explain how 
3 
these refinements will help identify potential issues well in advance of 
4 
experiencing demand/supply mismatches; manage the fixed costs commitments 
5 
made by the Companies; and provide a more thorough framework for the 
6 
Companies to consider alternatives that would have lower all-in costs to 
7 
customers.  
8 
Q.  
Was your testimony prepared by you or under your supervision?  
9 
A  
Yes.  
10

Are you sponsoring any exhibits?

A.  
Yes, I am sponsoring the following exhibits:

17 
III.   
Need for Revisions  
18 
Q.  
Have there been changes in market conditions over the last decade that have 
19 
affected natural gas utilities?   
20 
A.  
Yes. Over the past decade, two major trends have marked the evolution of the 
21 
natural gas business. These two major trends, viewed generally, have had and will 
22 


--- Page 7 ---
 
 
19-G-0309  
 
 
Direct Testimony of Gregory M. Lander 
19-G-0310                                                       
6 
 
continue to have profound impacts on gas distribution utilities, especially in the 
1 
northeastern United States. The first of these trends is the explosive growth in 
2 
natural gas supply from the Marcellus and Utica shales in the regions of 
3 
northeastern and southwestern Pennsylvania, southeastern Ohio and northern 
4 
West Virginia. The second is the dramatic increase in gas-fired electric 
5 
generation, especially in Ohio, Pennsylvania, New Jersey, New York and New 
6 
England. The second of these trends, driven by plentiful, low-priced natural gas 
7 
has placed strains on the legacy pipeline infrastructure serving the same region. 
8 
The dramatic growth in gas-fired generation has had a particular impact on the 
9 
legacy pipeline infrastructure especially during winter periods when both 
10 
domestic gas fueled heating needs and electric demands have placed simultaneous 
11 
demands on pipelines. In the past, Local Distribution Companies (“LDCs”) like 
12 
the Companies were the primary users of pipelines during the winter. Now, LDCs 
13 
and generators seek to use the same pipelines at the same hours of the day. As a 
14 
result, the flexibility that the LDCs used to have with respect to hourly takes is 
15 
being much more frequently restricted by the pipelines to the LDCs’ specified 
16 
contract rights. At the same time, because the loads of electric generators can be 
17 
as large as (or larger) than many of the LDCs’ take stations for whole swaths of 
18 
their distribution territories, these combined demands are a new and growing 
19 
factor that the industry has to cope with. Thus, LDC capacity planning that 
20 
previously may have viewed its planning criteria as a daily load, now has to 
21 
increasingly plan for peak hours of many more days in the winter than its “design 
22 


--- Page 8 ---
 
 
19-G-0309  
 
 
Direct Testimony of Gregory M. Lander 
19-G-0310                                                       
7 
 
day.” While in the past LDCs’ on-system peaking resources may have been used, 
1 
or planned to be used, during three to twenty days per winter, now may need to be 
2 
called upon throughout the winter to meet hourly demands that are in excess of 
3 
the LDCs’ hourly contract rights. The significance of this is that even though the 
4 
hours of use of on-system resources may increase, when looked at from an annual 
5 
utilization basis, may not exceed 3% to 6% of 8760 hours per year (i.e., 250 to 
6 
500 hours per year). Given such low load factors and given the cost of 
7 
incremental pipeline capacity, it simply may not make sense to commit to long-
8 
term capacity contracts in light of significant costs, the State’s climate policies, 
9 
and the Commission’s findings on non-pipeline alternatives.1 
10 
Q.  
Please explain the consequences of this market evolution.  
11 
A.  
In addition to the tightening of pipeline operations which have reduced LDCs’ 
12 
previous flexibility to take gas at a greater hourly rate than their strict contract 
13 
entitlements, producers and marketers have subscribed to large amounts of 
14 
capacity from supply areas both to and past large metropolitan demand centers, 
15 
and, in many cases, all the way from the supply areas to the U.S. Gulf coast to 
16 
supply Liquefied Natural Gas (“LNG”) export facilities. This increase in pipeline 
17 
capacity is a resource, which, if it can flow into (as opposed to past), LDC 
18 
distribution locations can be contracted for by LDCs based upon a price which 
19 
                                                     
1  Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and 
Regulations of Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corporation for Gas Service, Order 
Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal and Establishing Electric and Gas Rate Plan, Case 
No. 17-G-0460 et al. at page 75 (June 14, 2018) (finding that that non-pipeline 
alternatives should be “explored as a universal practice as an alternative to traditional 
investments.”).  


--- Page 9 ---
 
 
19-G-0309  
 
 
Direct Testimony of Gregory M. Lander 
19-G-0310                                                       
8 
 
makes it in the supplier’s interest to supply the LDC instead of taking the gas to 
1 
export. Such arrangements would have the effect of reducing the need to build 
2 
additional capacity from supply areas to market, and instead provide and plan for 
3 
the capacity to exist along the “last mile” (i.e., into and from the LDC take 
4 
stations). These combined facts—LDC short duration needs coupled with existing 
5 
capacity moving gas past the LDC demand centers—present an opportunity to 
6 
view capacity planning in a different way than ever before. Thus, a portfolio 
7 
approach, a combination of on-system resources to meet needle hourly peaks plus 
8 
contracted supply otherwise bound for export, have the effect of making LDC 
9 
capacity planning a multi-dimensional exercise. 
10 
Q.  
Has National Grid acknowledged the need for refinements to its gas supply 
11 
planning in other jurisdictions?  
12 
A.  
Yes. In a February 20, 2019 Joint Memorandum submitted to the Rhode Island 
13 
Public Utilities Commission, National Grid stated:  
14 
In the past, the [Long Range Plan] filings were not controversial and 
15 
tended to raise few complicated issues. But now, the Company needs 
16 
to plan in a way that assures adequate capacity and delivery security 
17 
under supply contracts, the magnitude and implications of which have 
18 
grown substantially. As a result, the current framework and template 
19 
for the Company’s long-range planning is no longer sufficient for an 
20 
appropriate regulatory review.2  
21 
 
22 
Q.  
Would you agree that these same concerns are relevant to National Grid’s 
23 
New York utilities?  
24 
A.  
Yes, the two trends I detailed above have profound impacts on gas distribution 
25 
utilities, both in the northeastern United States and in New York State.   
26 
                                                     
2  Exhibit__(GL-3) at page 2.

Q.  
Has the Commission recognized the need to review and assess policies, 
1 
programs, and regulations in light of these changes?  
2 
A.  
Yes. In a January 28, 2019 Statement, Chair John Rhodes directed DPS Staff to 
3 
“develop a report to review the changing market conditions that gave rise to Con 
4 
Edison’s decision [to institute a moratorium] and to develop recommendations to 
5 
make certain that utilities across the state are able to meet customer needs in a 
6 
manner consistent with the State’s energy goals.”3 The Statement also 
7 
acknowledges the need for utilities to help consumers conserve energy and to 
8 
solicit for natural gas supply alternatives.  My recommendations set forth below 
9 
will help the Commission and the Companies satisfy these objectives.   
10 
Q.  
Have there been any other recent developments in New York that compel 
11 
utilities to revisit their gas supply planning?  
12 
A.  
Yes. On July 18, 2019, Governor Cuomo signed into law the Climate Leadership 
13 
and Community Protection Act.4 The Act requires a reduction in statewide 
14 
greenhouse gas emissions by 85 percent by 2050 from 1990 levels, with an 
15 
additional goal of achieving net zero emissions across all sectors of the economy 
16 
by that time. The Act provides that in issuing approvals and decisions, all State 
17 
agencies, offices, authorities, and divisions “shall consider whether such decisions 
18

10 
 
are inconsistent with or will interfere with the attainment of the statewide 
1 
greenhouse gas emissions limits established in Article 75 of the Environmental 
2 
Conservation Law….”5   
3 
Q. 
Please explain the impact of this new legislation on gas supply planning.   
4 
A.  
This Act underscores the importance of thoughtful, transparent, and robust 
5 
planning. As New York proceeds forward on its path outlined in the legislation 
6 
there is a need for a transparent demonstration of the true demands of the system 
7 
and the all-in costs of meeting that demand with various resources, being mindful 
8 
not to lock-in greenhouse gas emissions from unnecessary long-lived and possibly 
9 
stranded infrastructure.  
10 
IV.  
Suggested Improvements to Gas Supply Planning 
11 
Q.  
Please provide an overview of the current gas supply planning process in 
12 
New York.  
13 
A.  
It is my understanding that the Commission opens a docket every year in order to 
14 
examine each gas utility’s supply plans. Each utility provides information 
15 
regarding their portfolio and purchasing strategies, anticipated portfolio changes, 
16 
gas supply portfolio details, among other issues.   
17 
Q.  
Have you reviewed the Companies’ gas supply submittal in Case No. 18-M-
18 
0272?  
19 
A.  
Yes, I have reviewed the Companies’ July 16, 2018 response to Staff’s Winter 
20 
Supply Review Data Request, attached to my testimony as Exhibit __ (GL-2), and 
21 
                                                     
5  CLCPA, Section 7(2).  


--- Page 12 ---
 
 
19-G-0309  
 
 
Direct Testimony of Gregory M. Lander 
19-G-0310                                                       
11 
 
the various updates and reports submitted by the Companies in that same 
1 
proceeding. In addition, I have reviewed the testimony of Elizabeth Arangio 
2 
submitted in these proceedings. 
3 
Q.  
Do you have any observations regarding these annual gas supply review 
4 
proceedings?  
5 
A.  
Yes. Although the Commission has broad discretion to review the long-range 
6 
programs of gas utilities,6 the Commission historically has not made any formal 
7 
public findings regarding the sufficiency of each gas utility’s supply plan in these 
8 
proceedings. Rather, it seems to be just a process between the utility and DPS 
9 
Staff. Moreover, they are not formatted in such a manner as would make it 
10 
possible to compare what is set forth in these planning documents and what 
11 
customers ultimately will be asked to pay. Intervenors historically have not had 
12 
discovery rights in these proceedings and there are no transcribed, on the record, 
13 
technical conferences, let alone a formal evidentiary hearing.   
14 
Q.  
How does this process in New York compare to other state commissions’ 
15 
review of gas utility supply plans, of which you are aware?  
16 
A.  
At present, based upon my knowledge, there is no “perfect” state process. 
17 
However, there are several states which require submissions of data, which if 
18 
combined, come close to the type of transparency that could and should be the 
19 
hallmark of an ideal process. For instance, in North Carolina, Piedmont Natural 
20 
                                                     
6   Public Service Law § 5(2) (the Commission has broad discretion to “encourage all 
persons and corporations subject to its jurisdiction to formulate and carry out long-
range programs, individually or cooperatively, for the performance of their public 
service responsibilities with economy, efficiency, and care for the public safety….”).

12 
 
Gas files historic and projected load duration curves and against such curves 
1 
presents its “resource stack” of pipeline capacity and on-system supplementals 
2 
(e.g., LNG and CNG) to demonstrate its resource sufficiency.7 In Massachusetts, 
3 
the utilities present design day demand (net of conservation and energy 
4 
efficiency) and against that they present their contractual8 and on-system resource 
5 
stack and identify surplus or deficit conditions with respect to that matching of 
6 
forecasted demand to contracted resources.9 In addition, many utilities that I am 
7 
aware of also conduct post-winter analyses of peak hourly takes at the take station 
8 
level and compare these experienced takes to their system model’s predictions of 
9 
takes under the experienced degree day conditions. In many cases this “post-
10 
action” analysis coupled with future demand growth projections identifies 
11 
potential hourly demand versus hourly contracted resource deficits.   
12 
In Rhode Island, the Division of Public Utilities and Carriers (i.e., the Staff) and 
13 
Narragansett Electric Company (a National Grid company) have proposed to 
14

13 
 
improve the information available in the gas supply planning process.10 One such 
1 
improvement is the presentation of the effective per unit fixed costs based upon 
2 
the planned load factor utilization of the individual transportation resource paths.  
3 
Adding to this effective (i.e., load factor sensitive) fixed cost, the modeled cost of 
4 
gas, generates an “all-in” cost presentation. The revised proposed protocol, set 
5 
forth in a February 20, 2019 Joint Memorandum, is attached to my testimony as 
6 
Exhibit__(GL-3).  
7 
Q.  
Do you have any recommendations to improve the Companies’ gas supply 
8 
planning process?  
9 
A.  
Yes, my recommendations fall into three general categories. First, I propose 
10 
changes to the process by which gas supply issues are addressed. These changes 
11 
would augment and supplement the current annual Staff review of gas supply 
12 
plans. Second, I propose changes to how the Commission should review and 
13 
consider gas supply information. In essence, each Company would be required to 
14 
submit a long range plan, which would form the basis for future cost recovery in a 
15 
gas cost reconciliation proceeding. Third, I propose changes to the types of 
16 
information that each Company should submit to better inform gas supply 
17 
decision making.  
18 
Q.  
Please explain your first category of changes to the process by which gas 
19 
supply issues are addressed.  
20 
                                                     
10  At present, in part because the protocol is pending before the Rhode Island Public 
Utilities Commission, the information is not available outside of the Division and the 
Company. 


--- Page 15 ---
 
 
19-G-0309  
 
 
Direct Testimony of Gregory M. Lander 
19-G-0310                                                       
14 
 
A.  
First of all, the process should be an annual process.  It should be an open process 
1 
with intervenors having discovery rights, and to the extent it is more 
2 
administratively efficient to have a series of technical conferences (following 
3 
discovery), then those should be transcribed. If needed, an evidentiary hearing 
4 
should be established. As the Commission has recently acknowledged, “[g]as 
5 
supply constraint solutions will need to involve greater visibility of the 
6 
distribution planning process to stakeholders and local communities, to enable 
7 
joint problem solving.”11 
8 
Q.  
Please explain your second category of changes to how the Commission 
9 
should review and consider gas supply information.   
10 
A.  
As a starting place, each Company should submit a long-range plan, which would 
11 
set forth projections of demand, by peak hour by Company operational “division” 
12 
and by day by “division.” Against that demand, the resources to meet that demand 
13 
should be set based upon the contracts and the on-system supply capabilities of 
14 
each Company. Each Company should then identify the cost of each resource 
15 
(fixed cost and projected12 or known13 variable costs) and the projected load 
16 
                                                     
11  Petition of Consolidated Edison Company of New York, Inc. for Approval of the 
Smart Solutions for Natural Gas Customers Program, Case No. 17-G-0606 at 35, 
Order Approving with Modification the Non-Pipeline Solutions Portfolio (February 7, 
2019).  
12  Here, “projected” would be in those cases where future costs are based upon market 
indices and projections of those future values. 
13  Here, “known” would exclude instances where negotiations have not been completed 
or where revealing “known” would impact negotiations for similarly situated 
transactions. 


--- Page 16 ---
 
 
19-G-0309  
 
 
Direct Testimony of Gregory M. Lander 
19-G-0310                                                       
15 
 
factor utilization of the resources so that all-in costs can be reviewed and 
1 
alternatives that might result in lower all-in cost be evaluated.   
2 
Q.  
Do the Companies provide any of this information already?  
3 
A. 
In Ms. Arangio’s testimony, this type of presentation was provided for certain 
4 
peak period resources. My recommendation is that it be done for all transportation 
5 
paths based upon modeled utilizations of the capacity paths. In addition, it would 
6 
be helpful to provide this presentation for design day, normal winter weather and 
7 
design winter weather. 
8 
Q.  
Once each Company submits a long range plan, what would follow?  
9 
A.  
An agreed-upon long range plan would become the basis for both the annual 
10 
(and/or semi-annual) gas cost reconciliation proceedings; and, for rate case 
11 
revenue requirement development. In the gas cost reconciliation proceedings, the 
12 
long range plan would provide the baseline. Differences between the baseline and 
13 
the actuals in the gas cost reconciliation proceeding would be evaluated as 
14 
“variances from plan.” This is in contrast to the Commission having to review the 
15 
gas cost reconciliation proceedings with information, resources, structures, and 
16 
costs that may not have been revealed previously.  
17 
Q.  
Please explain what you mean by “all-in cost.”  
18 
A.  
The “all-in cost” is determined by looking at the annual facilities’ fixed costs plus 
19 
commodity/O&M cost per unit of demand met taking into account the load factor 
20 
of the annual demand to be met.  Below I provide an example of “all-in” cost 
21 
calculations. 
22

12

14

15

17

6 
Q.  
Would these refinements lead to benefits?  
7 
A.  
A more robust planning process would lead to multiple benefits, including: (1) 
8 
identifying potential issues well in advance of experiencing demand/supply 
9 
mismatches; (2) a way to manage and contain the fixed cost commitments made 
10 
by the Companies, which will protect ratepayers against unreasonable financial 
11 
risk and protect the Companies against prudency risk from after-the-fact 
12 
regulatory challenges; and (3) a more thorough framework for the Companies to 
13 
consider alternatives that would have lower all-in costs to customers.

V.  
New York Facilities System  
15 
Q.  
Please provide an overview of the New York Facilities System.  
16 
A.  
As Ms. Arangio explains:  
17 
  
KEDNY and KEDLI are parties to an agreement with 
18 
Consolidated Edison Company of New York, Inc. (“Con Edison”) 
19 
concerning the ownership and operation of the New York Facilities 
20 
System (“NYFS”), the high pressure gas transmission system 
21 
serving the three downstate New York distribution companies.  
22 
The agreement permits the parties to contract for the transportation 
23 
and receipt of gas from various interstate pipelines that 
24 
interconnect with the NYFS, including Transcontinental Gas 
25 
Pipeline Company LLC (“Transco”), Texas Eastern Transmission 
26 
LP (“Texas Eastern”), Iroquois Gas Transmission System LP

(“Iroquois”), 
and

Company 
1 
(“Tennessee”).17   
2 
 
3 
Q.  
How do the Companies and Con Edison plan for the needs of the NYFS?  
4 
 
5 
A.  
My understanding is that the Companies and Con Edison prepare joint hydraulic 
6 
models of the NYFS,18 file an updated appendix annually with the Commission, 
7 
and discuss the modeling results from time-to-time with Staff at their request.19 
8 
Q. 
Do you have any recommendations to improve the planning process for the 
9 
NYFS?  
10 
A.  
Yes. I recommend that the Commission undertake a proceeding where the 
11 
planning assumptions of the NYFS are formalized and integrated into the more 
12 
general, downstate energy planning process for the NYFS members. The 
13 
Commission may even find that it appropriate to view and regulate the NYFS as a 
14 
functioning independent not-for-profit gas grid operator. This would entail 
15 
formally identifying which facilities of the downstate gas utilities be contained 
16 
within such organization, defining cost allocation and cost recovery mechanisms 
17 
for the organization and finally, defining the goals and charges of such 
18 
organization in the planning, operating, and optimal execution of the State’s 
19 
energy and climate policies. 
20 
Q.  
Does this conclude your testimony? 
 
21 
A.  
Yes.

Greg Lander, President 
Skipping Stone LLC  
 
 
 
 
 
 
Professional Summary: 
As President of Skipping Stone Inc., Greg Lander is responsible for Strategic 
Consulting in the mergers and acquisition arena with numerous clients within the 
energy industry. Generally recognized in the energy industry as an expert, he has 
advised and/or given testimony at numerous Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
(FERC), State, arbitration, and legal proceedings on behalf of clients and has advised 
as well as initiated standards formation before the Gas Industry Standards Board 
(GISB) (predecessor to the North American Energy Standards Board (NAESB)).  As 
Founder, President, and Chief Technology Officer of TransCapacity Limited 
Partnership, he was responsible for conceiving, planning, managing, and designing 
Transaction Coordination Systems utilizing Electronic Data Interchange (EDI) 
between trading partners. As a founding member of GISB, he assisted in establishing 
protocols and standards within the Business Practices, Interpretations and Triage 
Subcommittees.  
Professional Accomplishments: 
 
Handled all Due Diligence for purchaser (Loews Corp) in acquisitions of two 
interstate pipelines, one natural gas storage complex, and ethylene distribution 
and transmission systems (Texas Gas Transmission, Gulf South Pipeline, Petal 
Storage, Petrologistics, and Chevron Ethylene Pipeline) most in excess of $1 
Billion.  Developed purchaser’s business case model, including rate/revenue 
models, forward contract renewal models, export basis modeling and revenue 
models, and operating cost and capex models. Coordinated Engineering and 
Environmental Due Diligence Teams integrating findings and assessments into 
final Diligence Reports. 
 
Assisted major electric retailer in 9 states with business case development for 
entry into North Eastern U.S. Commercial & Industrial natural gas marketing 
business.  Identified market share of incumbents; retail registration process, 
billing processes; utility data exchange rules and procedures and developed 
estimates of addressable market by utility.  
 
Handled all economic Due Diligence for purchaser of large minority stake in 
Southern Star Gas Pipeline.  Developed purchaser’s business case model, 
including rate/revenue models and forward contract renewal models, assessed 
potential competitive by-pass of asset located in “pipeline alley”, developed 
revenue models and operating cost and capex models. Coordinated 
Engineering, Pipeline Integrity, and Environmental Due Diligence Teams 
integrating findings and assessments into final Diligence Reports. 
 
Developed post-acquisition integration plans for inter-operability and alterations 
to system operations to take advantage of opportunities presented by

4. Identify your source for heating degree day (HDD) data, including the specific weather 
data points used for forecast purposes.  Describe your source and/or your calculation of 
design day and design winter data (i.e. calculated from normal usage or an actual 
historic period).  Identify the time periods used to develop usage per HDD for both 
design and normal usage, and explain the frequency of updates.  If 30 years of data is 
not being used for design, please explain why.  Please explain how usage per HDD for 
the peak period is calculated and verified. 
Downstate NY (KEDNY and KEDLI)  
 
The Downstate NY (KEDNY and KEDLI) design standards are based on the KNYC Central 
Park, NYC, NY weather station.  The Company developed its normal year (4,587 HDD) 
based on 30 years of weather data from July 1985 to June 2015.  This normal year was 
approved by the Commission in Cases 16-G-0058 and 16-G-0059. 
 
To develop its normal year, the Company calculated the monthly HDD values as well as the 
standard deviation of the daily HDDs in each month.  The Company then developed its 
normal year (‘Typical Meteorological Year’) by choosing historical months whose monthly 
totals and standard deviations approximated the mean monthly HDD and mean standard 
deviations observed.  The Company then tuned each month’s data to achieve the exact mean 
monthly total and mean standard deviation.  
 
Using the mean and standard deviation of the annual HDD data, the Company established its 
design year standard of 5,370 HDD as a once-in-40-year probability of occurrence. The 
Downstate NY Peak Day remains at 65 HDD. 
 
Upstate NY (NMPC) 
  
In prior years, the Company has used the arithmetic average of daily weather data from the 
KSYR, Syracuse, NY and the KALB, Albany, NY weather stations.  Beginning with this 
submission, the NMPC design standards are based on the KSYR, Syracuse, NY weather 
station for its Central division and the KALB, Albany, NY weather station for its Eastern 
division.  The Company developed its normal years (6,588 HDD for Syracuse and 6,539 
HDD for Albany) based on 30 years of weather data from July 1986 to June 2016.  This 
separation of weather station data was implemented as part of the Joint Proposal in Case 17-
G-0202. 
 
The Company uses the same approach as described above in the Downstate NY description 
to establish its Upstate NY Typical Meteorological Years. 
 
Using the mean and standard deviation of the annual HDD data, the Company established its 
design year standard of 7,462 HDD for Syracuse and 7,400 HDD for Albany using the same 
probability of occurrence used in prior years’ KSYR and KALB average data: once-in-19.07 
years. The Upstate NY Peak Days for both Syracuse and Albany remain at 75 HDD.

5 
 
Usage per HDD 
 
The Company’s wholesale (city gate) forecast begins with aligning its regression equations 
relating its actual daily send out observations to actual HDD for the most recent 12-month 
period.  For this forecast, that period is April 2017 – March 2018.  There are independent 
regression equations for KEDNY, KEDLI, NMPC-Eastern, and NMPC-Central.  Each 
regression equation is a segmented equation that identifies the point at which space heating 
equipment begins to be used.  Each regression equation also captures the observed usage 
changes between weekday and weekend, as well as the effect of prior days’ HDD on each 
day’s send out.  The accuracy of the Company’s wholesale forecasts is then tracked 
throughout the heating season in the event that actual customer demand deviates from 
forecasted demand so that appropriate actions can be taken. 
5. Describe the load forecasting tools used to develop the above forecasts.  Indicate how all 
natural gas efficiency programs, Demand Response Programs, Microgrids, and Non- 
Pipe Alternatives (NPA) conducted by your company, contractors or the New York 
State Energy Research and Development Authority (NYSERDA) have been 
incorporated into these forecasts and your capacity planning.  Provide a summary of 
the projected energy savings and the actual savings realized to date.  How are these 
savings translated into the normal usage projection in Table 3? 
The Company’s load growth forecasts are based on econometric/statistical forecast models 
developed for each residential, commercial/industrial, and multifamily rate class. Two 
different models were developed for each class: (1) a model to forecast the number of 
customers, and (2) a model to forecast use per customer. In the number of customer models, 
the independent variables are a combination of time trends, population, households, 
employment, and gas and oil prices. In the models calculating use per customer, the 
independent variables are a combination of time trends, heating degree days, population, 
housing stock, income, employment, unemployment, gross domestic product, and gas and oil 
prices. The Company obtained the historical data and forecasts of the independent variables 
from a leading economic research and forecasting firm and energy prices from the U.S. 
Department of Energy, Energy Information Administration, and used this data to develop the 
econometric models and generate load forecasts. 
 
The Company’s historical sales data includes the impact of actual energy efficiency savings 
from both NYSERDA and Company-sponsored energy efficiency programs. 
   
In 2016, the Company’s Energy Efficiency department began its ETIP (“Energy Efficiency 
Transition Implementation Plan).  In prior years, the Company ran its EEPS (“Energy 
Efficiency Portfolio Standard”) programs.  The table below provides the Company’s annual 
savings under EEPS for 2009-2015 and its ETIP program actuals for 2016 and 2017 are 
presented.

9. Describe the storage injection plan for this injection season and highlight any 
modifications from the prior year plan. 
The Company’s basic injection strategy is to begin injecting small volumes in the month of 
April followed by levelized injections from May through September, then tapering down 
injections in October. The Company’s transportation assets available for injections may be 
limited in April and October, as they may be needed to meet customer requirements. 
Additionally, the Company requires flexibility in these shoulder months to adjust to changes 
in weather.  
 
After each month, inventories and potential ratchets are reviewed and adjustments are made 
to continue injections. The target inventory level for most storage fields is 100% with some 
exceptions found in the following table. Additional injections (“downswing”) may be needed 
to accommodate warm days in November. Below is a summary of targets for those storage 
fields that do not get filled to 100% along with changes from 2017 to 2018.

10. Provide the method(s) used by the Company to determine or ensure the least cost 
dispatch when the heating season is warmer than normal. 
The Company’s fundamental approach to supply procurement (i.e., serving customers at the 
lowest possible cost) is the same under design, normal, and warmer-than-normal conditions. 
On a monthly basis, the Company first estimates minimum daily customer requirements from 
a combination of the annual gas load forecast and actual usage over the past several years. 
The Company then dispatches a least-cost combination of storage withdrawals and base load 
flowing supplies to meet, but not exceed, minimum customer requirements, while also 
satisfying anticipated operational needs. The Company retains enough capacity to meet 
normal and maximum daily customer requirements, while also incorporating flexibility to 
meet operational requirements and to adapt to changing pricing dynamics.  
 
The Company seeks to economically displace storage withdrawals with flowing supplies 
when the costs of flowing supplies are less than the storage inventory cost. The opportunity 
to economically displace storage withdrawals with less expensive flowing supplies can occur 
when the heating season is warmer than normal, normal, or colder than normal. The 
Company will only displace storage withdrawals to the extent that it can still satisfy must 
turn requirements pursuant to its storage agreements. Please see the Company’s response to 
Question 11 regarding must turn requirements. 
 
Warmer-than-normal weather may also present the Company with additional opportunities 
for optimization of excess capacity. The Company’s response to Question 22 details how the 
Company makes use of this excess capacity to reduce customer costs.

13 
 
Issue 3 
Please provide the following information related to your company’s operations and optimization 
procedures: 
12. A send out schedule (or curve) for forecasting requirements under the varying 
conditions that are considered in developing the estimates (e.g., temperature, wind, 
weekend/weekday, etc.). 
The Company performs linear regression analysis with three years of historical data for 
Downstate NY and four years of historical data for Upstate NY to develop predictive send 
out equations each month. The Company has found that limiting regressions to three years of 
data provides the most accurate estimate of send out for Downstate NY, as using additional 
years of data tends to skew the send out lower due to system growth. Upstate NY has not 
experienced the same level of growth as compared to Downstate NY, so regressing with an 
additional year of data provides for a more accurate forecast for this territory. 
 
The send out equations correlate daily firm demand (sales and transportation) for all 
Companies and TC/Interruptible demand (sales and transportation) for Downstate NY to the 
following variables:  
 
• The gas day’s predicted 24-hour average temperature 
• Prior and two prior gas days’ 24-hour average temperatures 
• Wind speed 
• Day type (to differentiate a weekday from a weekend day or holiday) 
• Day of month (to differentiate the beginning-of-month versus end-of-month for some 
shoulder period months) 
• Cloud Cover (Upstate NY only) 
 
Not all of the factors listed above are needed to predict daily send out in each month. For 
example, during the summer, send out may not correlate strongly to wind speed. The use of 
cloud cover is unique to the Upstate NY equation, as it has proven to be a material variable in 
determining load during the winter season. The Downstate NY region does not experience 
the same material impact of cloud cover.  
 
Gas Supply Planning monitors performance of the equations on an ongoing basis and makes 
adjustments as required to ensure the continued accuracy of the forecast.

• Exhibit 13: Equations For Predicting November 2018 Firm & TC Throughput

19. A description of the long and short term forecasting process used for gas dispatch 
purposes. Include all weather services and a description of any in-house software 
utilized. Please explain how accurate your short term forecasts were during the 2017-18 
heating season by using a back cast after the actual weather is known. 
Long term forecasting is done per the methodology described in response to Question 5 
above. 
 
Short term forecasting is a joint effort between Energy Procurement and Gas Control. Both 
areas use Weather Services International (WSI) for weather forecasts. WSI provides a 10-day 
forecast, which is updated five times per day. WSI also includes actual weather for the 
previous day. The data include hourly temperatures, high & low temperatures with the time 
of day, wind speed, and cloud cover. The information is transmitted via e-mail and FTP. 
Additionally, the Company receives zone forecasts for the Company’s service areas in a text 
format from Meteorlogix. 
 
Two methodologies are used for short term forecasting: 
 
• Matching Days Selection: This is an application built into the Company’s SCADA 
System. 
• Rate Curve forecasting - Energy Procurement  provides a rate curve calculation each 
month for each of National Grid’s service areas, as described in response to Question 
12 above. SCADA Support personnel enter these calculations into the SCADA 
System. The weather data from WSI are loaded into the system and applied to the 
send out equations. Send out forecasts for each area are updated five times daily with 
scheduled weather updates.

b. Please describe the methodology utilized to determine the mandatory capacity 
release to the marketers. Indicate how this compares with the methodology utilized 
to determine capacity required for firm sales customers. 
The Company plans portfolios for all core customers to reliably meet their requirements 
under design conditions. The portfolios are comprised of year-round transportation 
contracts, storage contracts, and peaking contracts. The deliverability of these assets 
corresponds to customer requirements as closely as possible. The Company then releases 
capacity to marketers pursuant to the descriptions below. The remaining assets are used 
to meet the requirements of firm sales customers and non-core customers and to balance 
system load. 
 
Downstate NY 
 
ESCOs serving customers under the Core Monthly Balancing Program must participate 
in the Company’s Mandatory Capacity Program subject to the terms and conditions of the 
KEDLI and KEDNY tariffs and the Gas Transportation Operational Procedures Manual. 
The Mandatory Capacity Program consists of four capacity tiers: 1) Tier 1 – Pipeline 
Capacity Release, 2) Tier 2A – Physical Storage Release, 3) Tier 2B – Retail Access 
Storage and 4) Tier 3 – Peaking Service if an ESCO's Tier 1 and 2 allocations cannot 
meet estimated requirements. The Company will use the aggregate Base and Slope 
components of each ESCO pool to provide the ESCO with sufficient portfolio assets to 
meet the design weather requirements of each ESCO’s pool. These portfolio assets will 
reflect (on a proportional basis) the mix of transportation and storage assets used by the 
Company to serve its firm sales customers. ESCOs will also be offered access to 
additional storage assets every April in the form of a physical release of storage capacity 
from the Company’s Gulf Coast Storage assets.  
 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation 
 
Each month the Marketer has the ability to enroll or drop customers from their pool. 
After the deadline for enrollments is done National Grid’s system Transportation 
Services Application (TSA) receives a file from National grid’s system Customer Service 
System (CSS). The file from CSS contains customer’s base & slopes for each Marketer. 
TSA takes all the Base/Slopes to calculate their MPDQ and then does a calculation to 
sum all of the Marketers customers MPDQ and calculates the Marketer’s Pool’s total 
peak day allocation.

f. Please describe how your company determines the daily delivery quantities (DDQ) 
provided to marketers each month for their daily delivery requirements. Provide a 
sample calculation. 
Downstate NY 
 
A Customer's Core DDQ is calculated by multiplying forecasted HDDs by a customer’s 
Slope and adding the product to the customer’s Base. The Core DDQ is adjusted by a 
UFG factor as an allowance for losses incurred in the process of delivery to the 
customer’s metered facilities. Each ESCO’s aggregate Core DDQ is rounded to the 
nearest whole dekatherm and posted to the Electronic Bulletin Board (EBB) as the 
ESCO’s daily requirement. 
 
KEDNY Example: 
 
If the Projected Temperature is 42 degrees, 65-42 = 23 HDD for that specific day. The 
UFG = 1.0227 and the customer’s base is 50 and slope is 3, then the formula is: 
 
DDQ = 1.0227 * (50 + (3 * (IF (Forecasted Temperature > 65, 0, 65 – Forecasted 
Temperature) 
DDQ = 1.0227 * (50 + (3 * (23))) 
DDQ = 121 Dth for that day and it’s posted directly into our EBB for the specific 
marketer. 
 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation 
 
NMPC refers to the DDQ as the Daily Contract Quantity (DCQ). 
 
DCQ = (HDD X (Thermal Response X Monthly Thermal Adjustment Factor)) + daily 
base load.   
 
If this calculation produces DCQ's greater than a marketer’s DTI FT release, National 
Grid will only expect a nomination that is less than or equal to their DTI FT release. 
 
Thermal Adjustment Factor:  
 
The percentages listed in the normal response column below are applicable when HDD 
are less than or equal to the HDD threshold. When HDD are greater than the threshold, 
the cold response percentages are utilized. The Company believes this adjustment 
provides a more accurate estimate of customer requirements at colder temperatures 
during shoulder months compared with no adjustment.

Example:  
 
If the Projected Temperature is 42 degrees, 65 - 42 = 23 HDD for that specific day. The 
customer’s base is 50 and slope is 3, and it is September. The formula is: 
 
DCQ = (23 X (3 X 100%)) + 50 
DCQ = 119 Dth for that day  
 
In Upstate NY, the Monthly Balancing ESCOs Unaccounted For Gas (UFG) is only 
included in a true up report generated by National Grid’s website Transportation Service 
Application (TSA). The ESCO’s Actual usage will be trued up to the ESCOs actual 
DCQs by billing cycle on a monthly basis. Cashouts will be determined using the average 
of the Gas Daily DTI North Point / Mid-Point, plus DTI Fuel, plus DTI’s Commodity 
Charge for each period for which the DCQ’s were delivered. When the Gas Daily DTI 
North Point/Mid-Point is not posted, the Gas Daily DTI South Point/Mid-Point will be 
used.

24. Description and status of efforts to verify customer alternative fuel availability and 
equipment testing, including:  
a. Methods utilized to verify dual-fuel customers' capabilities, including power 
generation customers. 
 
NMPC, KEDNY, and KEDLI hold annual tests at the beginning of each winter heating 
season to verify that dual-fuel customers are capable of switching from natural gas to 
their alternate fuel.  With regards to power generators, KEDNY and KEDLI have a 
process to confirm that there are five days of alternate fuel on hand. 
Exhibit__(GL-2)
Page 25 of 174 


--- Page 57 ---
Case 18-M-0272 
2018-19 National Grid Winter Supply Review 
Submission Date: July 16, 2018 
Version # 1 
 
26 
 
 
Field visits are made to all customers who fail to switch to their alternate fuel during the 
test to determine the cause of failure.  These field visits are conducted as soon as possible 
following the test to ensure the necessary remedies are put in place prior to the onset of 
extreme winter cold. 
 
Currently, NMPC sends an Alternative Fuel Source Verification (“AFSV”) form to each 
non-firm customer, requiring the customer to identify their alternative fuel source.  If the 
AFSV is not returned, NMPC presumes the customer uses No. 2 fuel oil as their alternate 
fuel.  NMPC then sends each customer an Alternate Fuel Source Requirements (“AFSR”) 
form that sets forth the alternative fuel source storage capacity requirements for their 
alternate fuel.  The customer is required to return the AFSR form specifying its total on-
site alternative fuel storage capacity.  The customer must select one of the following three 
options to satisfy the alternative fuel source requirements: 
1. Fill on-site storage and enter a contractual relationship with a fuel supplier (if 
necessary); 
2. Curtail operations to meet the available on-site storage; or 
3. Shut down operations 
 
Beginning in the Winter of 2017/2018, pursuant to the terms of the Joint Proposal in 
Cases 16-G-0058 and 16-G-0059, KEDNY and KEDLI required customers to provide 
affidavits confirming that they have alternative fuel supply contracts in place for the 
upcoming winter heating season and that such contracts provide for alternative fuel 
replenishment on an as-needed basis.  The affidavits also include information regarding 
on-site storage facilities, total storage capacity, and estimated number of peak days of 
storage.  This requirement was the first year of a three-year phase-in period. The 
Company will collect affidavits at random from one-third of its non-firm customer base 
each year.  New affidavits will be required from individual customers every three years.  
Customers will be responsible for providing new affidavits if there are any changes to the 
affidavits originally submitted prior to the three year renewal.

KEDNY/KEDLI TC customers with automatic switchover equipment will be switched to an

location to identify the reason for the burn and to repair any problems with National Grid 
equipment.  If customers’ burn gas for reasons associated with customer equipment or low 
levels of alternate fuel, the customer will be penalty billed for unauthorized gas use.  If 
deemed necessary for reliability purposes, such customers will be disconnected from gas 
service.

The reason for the moratorium is operational in nature. During a peak hour, East Gate 
requirements exceed the allowable flow rate from DETI. The Company has utilized its 
TGP city gate in South Albany to offset DETI flows, but is limited in takeaway capacity 
at that location. The Albany Loop Project, scheduled to be constructed in the next 3-5 
years, will increase takeaway from that location. Until then, the Company will look to 
CNG to help manage peak hour requirements. 
 
The Company is currently evaluating the need to expand the moratorium to include other 
customer classes as well. 
 
Downstate NY 
 
The Company is closely monitoring the timeline for the NESE project and associated 
approvals. Further delays may impact new firm customers.

e. If you serve customers in the New York City area, describe your analysis and the 
impacts to your system of the city’s anticipated proposal to require larger buildings 
to convert from heavy fuel oils (#4 and #6) to lighter oil (#2 and bio-fuels) and/or 
natural gas for space heating. 
Under the NYC Clean Heat program, National Grid has converted nearly all of the 
approximately 800 buildings in its service territory from No. 4/6 oil to natural gas. As a 
result, customer requirements have grown and infrastructure to support these conversions 
has been installed where needed.  
 
The City Clean Heat program deadline to phase out of all No. 6 heating oil was June 
30th, 2015. To date, the NYC Department of Environmental Protection has achieved 
99.8% compliance with the regulation. The deadline for the phase out of all No. 4 heating 
oil is January 1st, 2030. At this point the City has achieved it goals and is focusing on 
pursuing other carbon reductions options.

date is the Village of East Hills totaling 80,000 feet of main with 500 services installed to 
date. The Company is actively engaged with six large projects totaling 160,000 feet of 
main. To complement these efforts the Company is pursuing smaller main extensions 
when new inquiries meet NEP density requirements.

m. How do you determine the success or failure of your hedging program? 
n. Please provide internal reporting, oversight, and audit structure of your hedging 
program. 
The hedging program reporting and oversight is documented in the Company’s US 
Energy Commodity Risk Management Policy. 
 
National Grid’s US based energy procurement activities are managed under the direction 
of National Grid Plc’s Finance Committee. The Finance Committee authorized the 
Energy Procurement Risk Management Committee (“EPRMC”) and delegated authority 
to focus on energy risk, metrics, energy strategies, financial impacts and other 
opportunities to the EPRMC.  The EPRMC provides Vice President level review of 
Strategies, with a focus on market risks, inclusive of price, credit, operational, liquidity 
and reputational risks and Policy compliance. The EPRMC serves to administer a 
consistent and comprehensive sanctioning process for such Strategies and commitments 
across the organization. The EPRMC has delegated some of its authority to the 
Commodity Management Committee (“CMC”).  The EPRMC appoints the CMC 
members and chairperson.  The CMC provides detailed review of Strategies, products 
and new opportunities with a focus on energy risk, metrics, financial impacts and 
opportunities. The CMC conducts research to understand market rules, regulatory 
Exhibit__(GL-2)
Page 45 of 174 


--- Page 77 ---
Case 18-M-0272 
2018-19 National Grid Winter Supply Review 
Submission Date: July 16, 2018 
Version # 1 
 
46 
 
requirements, customer needs, risks, barriers to entry, logistical requirements, 
competition, deal economics, risk measurement and valuation requirements, all related 
requirements and exposures (including credit, liquidity, tax and legal), and the impact of 
current and proposed Strategies on existing controls and limits, while administering a 
consistent and comprehensive sanctioning process for such Strategies and commitments 
across the organization. 
 
The Company reviews the hedging program and highlights any changes with the NYPSC 
on an annual basis.  The Company has a comprehensive internal approval process to 
authorize a hedge strategy and maintain oversight requirements. Prior to execution, a 
hedging strategy is documented with details that may include: risks to be hedged, 
volumetric targets, duration and cadence of hedge plan transactions, and types of 
instruments to be used. This documentation is then reviewed and approved by the CMC 
prior to going to the EPRMC for approval and authorization.  
 
The Company employs the industry standard best practice of an independent three-office 
model: 
• The Front Office develops and executes the hedge strategies hedges in accordance 
with the prevailing policies and strategies by entering into transactions with 
counterparties to mitigate natural gas price volatility;  
• Risk Management is part of the Middle Office and maintains the overall control 
environment and assesses compliance with the Company’s risk policy.  The 
Middle Office reports through the Treasury organization and provides a 
significant level of control and independent policing of the Front Office’s 
activities.  The Middle Office confirms all trades with counterparties and monitors 
the risk exposures of the deals, as well as verifying approved strategies are 
executed in accordance with the plan; and  
• The Accounting Department is part of the Back Office whose functions include 
processes in support of the Front Office, such as accounting, invoicing, check-
outs, actualization, accounts receivable and payable, and financial reporting.  The 
Back Office is also independent of both the Front and Middle Offices and reports 
up through the Finance organization. 
 
Additionally, the Company’s internal auditing department has performed audits to ensure 
compliance, for example, with Sarbanes-Oxley.

Local production is currently less than 1% of the Company’s system throughput. The Fresh 
Kills Landfill, located in Staten Island, is currently the only source of local production for all 
three of National Grid’s NY LDCs. The landfill is owned by the City of New York. KEDNY 
has a supply agreement with the Landfill for up to 6,500 Dth/day, but there is no contractual 
minimum daily quantity. Landfill supply is directly fed into the distribution system. 
 
The Company’s use of local production has not changed over the past several years; the 
Company continues to purchase any supply the Landfill can provide. There is no forecast of 
Landfill production, but Gas System Operations communicates with the Landfill regularly 
and is notified of maintenance work and outages. When the Landfill is unable to sell supply, 
the Company replaces planned volumes with contingency supplies.

and the current. 
b. Changes in system operations from last year including how gas-fired electric 
generators’ needs and behavior during last winter impacted your distribution 
system operations. 
During this past winter season Gas Control initiated several Operational Flow Orders to 
control Generator behavior consistent their contractual tariffs. There were multiple 
instances where Generator behavior was inconsistent with the needs of our operational 
needs to serve our firm ratepayers and these have been addressed through penalty billing 
consistent with National Grids Tariff. 
c. Need for distribution system facilities improvements. 
There are no system improvements needed to support power generation based on the 
sourcing of gas and level of service contracted. Should power generators wish to modify 
their points of delivery or level of service, additional facility improvements may be 
required.  At this point in time all of the gas system reinforcement projects identified are 
to support the needs of our firm gas ratepayer. 
d. Available information on generators’ upstream capacity arrangements. 
 
e. Relationship between your company and its electric, steam operations or affiliate-
owned generation, including any peaking service agreements.

f. Distributed Generation/CHP, including any micro-grid applications, and their 
impact on peak design-day forecasting.  List all known planned projects and 
locations on the system. 
 
In the majority of National Grid’s New York service territory, NYSERDA is the service 
provider for the programs that offer customers incentives for CHP projects.  LIPA 
(PSEG) customers for the past few years are now eligible for CHP programs, which are 
modeled after the NYSERDA programs. Various market factors such as spark spread, 
cost of land, electrically constrained areas, and interest in resilient generation has resulted 
in most of the CHP growth in the Downstate NY.  Accordingly, the Company has a 
significant amount of interest and dedicated resources to promote the deployment of CHP 
in Downstate NY.  In Upstate NY, the Company has fewer CHP projects and overall 
growth in DG capacity has been from solar.

2018-19 National Grid Winter Supply Review 
Submission Date: July 16, 2018 
Version # 1 
 
54 
 
After a decade of rapid growth from the inception of natural gas vehicle programs through 
the 1990s, steady growth followed, fueled by federal and state tax incentives for vehicle 
conversions and fueling stations. Natural gas sales for use in NGVs then leveled off due to 
the expiration of those incentives and the loss of key product offerings for high fuel-use 
vehicle platforms. More recently, the NGV industry continues to enjoy the release of new 
and upgraded offerings and new market entrants. As expected, natural gas sales for NGV use 
rose again due to the sustained low price of natural gas relative to conventional fuels and the 
reintroduction of state and federal tax credits for the use of natural gas fuel. However, recent 
dramatic reductions in petroleum prices have again slowed growth.  The Company’s forecast 
is for flat to slight declining volumes. 
37.  Have you been approached by or had any discussions with outside entities regarding 
the construction of compressed natural gas (CNG) or liquefied natural gas (LNG) 
fueling stations? If so, please explain.  What impediments do you see for the expansion 
of CNG and LNG transportation? 
Customers and other outside entities have approached the Company regarding the 
construction of CNG fueling stations for transportation uses and more recently in upstate 
New York and Long Island for the loading of CNG trailers to serve stationary facilities. 
These discussions typically involve requests by customers for technical advice on the 
installation of such facilities and/or the availability of natural gas at potential fueling station 
sites. 
 
The major impediments for the expansion of both CNG and LNG for transportation use 
include the lack of existing fueling demand, high incremental costs for vehicles, the high cost 
of installing fueling stations on customer sites, the small number of publicly available fueling 
stations, the uncertainty of future petroleum and natural gas prices and the recent significant 
advances in electric vehicle market development and consumer awareness.  Recent declines 
in gasoline and diesel pricing has slowed but not stopped CNG project development.  For 
LNG as a transportation fuel, despite the effective repeal of the LNG moratorium outside 
New York City, the inability to obtain a permit for intrastate transportation of LNG 
combined with state and local permit requirements for storage under development effectively 
preclude significant growth. Finally, at this time the Company does not foresee the 
availability of gas as a significant impediment to expansion of these services, however there 
are areas of our distribution system where main reinforcements may be necessary in which 
case the Company works collaboratively with station developers to optimize the overall 
fueling station and manage costs.

Dear Customer: 
 
The purpose of this letter is to notify you that on November 16, 2017 National Grid (“Company”) will perform a test 
to confirm your ability to comply with the interruption provisions of the Company’s Temperature Controlled (“TC”) 
tariff. 
 
1. The annual gas interruption test is scheduled for November 16, 2017, beginning at 6:00 a.m.  For customers 
with remote switch over capability, National Grid will remotely switch your dual-fuel equipment for up to eight 
(8) hours. All other customers must remain off natural gas from the time they receive an alarm to switch over 
from gas until such time they receive an alarm notifying them that they can switch back to natural gas. It is 
imperative that your dual-fuel system be in good working order and that you have adequate alternate fuel on 
hand to sustain this interruption. If you have any equipment issues, it is imperative that you contact the 
Company within one (1) hour of when you or your operating personnel become aware of any equipment 
issues. 
 
2. Please promptly notify National Grid at 1-718-403-3134 of any condition that may prevent your equipment 
from switching to an alternate fuel. 
 
National Grid would like to take this opportunity to remind you of the tariff provisions for TC service: 
 
1. PLEASE NOTE National Grid reserves the right to raise the designated interruption temperature (for example, 
from 15°F to 20°F or higher) at any time as may be required, in National Grid’s sole judgment, to maintain 
system integrity or for other operational consideration. 
   
2. As a TC customer, you are required to stop burning natural gas when the outdoor temperature reaches your 
designated interruption temperature. If you have automatic switchover capability, your equipment will switch 
automatically from natural gas to an alternate fuel when the outdoor temperature falls to your thermostat’s 
designated temperature (15° F).  If you do not have automatic switchover capability, you must manually 
switch your equipment to an alternate fuel when the outdoor temperature falls to the designated temperature 
for manual equipment, (15° F). In addition, please be aware that the Company will initiate a remote switchover 
and send an alarm that manual equipment must be switched any time National Grid receives a report that the 
temperature has reached 15° F at LaGuardia Airport. National Grid may also initiate a remote switchover or 
send an alarm requesting that you switch your equipment manually in the event of a system reliability or 
supply issue regardless of temperature. However, customers in the Northern Queens area may be interrupted 
at temperatures higher than 15° F. After this remote switchover, when National Grid receives a report that the 
temperature has reached 20° F, customers with automatic switchover equipment will be switched back to 
natural gas and all others will receive an alarm indicating that they can manually switch their equipment back 
to burn natural gas. 
 
3. Your dual-fuel equipment and controls must be in working order. In addition, you must have a minimum ten 
(10) day supply of alternate fuel on hand. The “on hand” requirement can be satisfied by either storing the full 
10 day fuel requirement onsite, or through a combination of onsite storage and a delivery contract with a fuel 
supplier for the difference. Whenever National Grid becomes aware that a Customer’s equipment is incapable 
of switching over to an alternate fuel and/or that the Customer does not have an adequate supply of alternate 
fuel on hand, the Company will notify the Customer that the violation must be corrected within ten (10) 
calendar days. It will be the responsibility of the Customer to contact the Company and provide proof that the 
violation has been corrected. With regard to an inadequate fuel situation and/or equipment failure, Customers 
who had not supplied the Company with such proof will be subject to a non-compliance charge as stated in 
the Company’s TC tariff. The non-compliance charge will cease the day the Company receives satisfactory 
proof that the violation has been corrected. 
 
4. National Grid must be permitted to install, access, inspect and repair remote meter reading and fuel switching 
devices and associated controls located on your premises as required to assure proper operating and 
monitoring of your system. It is the Customer’s responsibility to avoid damage to or disconnection of the 
Company’s equipment. Any such event must be reported to the Company immediately. 
Exhibit__(GL-2)
Page 108 of 174 


--- Page 140 ---
5. National Grid has a communications system that will enable you to receive information regarding your 
account. This system provides real-time communications to interruptible customers regarding their natural gas 
service, including service interruption notifications. In addition to receiving traditional automated telephone 
calls, the system will allow you to receive messages via email, text and facsimile. You can register by visiting 
https://ngrid.myenergysites.com/nonfirm-communication. 
 
6. During each winter season, any Customer who does not interrupt gas usage when required to do so, 
including during system-wide tests, will be notified that they have violated the requirements of the tariff.  
Please provide prompt access to National Grid representatives when they come to inspect your equipment.  
  
7. Any failure to interrupt gas usage when required to do so will result in penalties and/or changes in service.  
Any gas consumed (above the minimum tolerance level established in the tariff) will result in unauthorized use 
charges as provided for in the Company’s TC tariff. In addition, if proof is not provided within ten (10) calendar 
days that the violation has been remedied, your account will also incur non-compliance charges. 
a. Customers who receive two (2) violations within one (1) winter season (November through March), will 
have their account transferred, effective no later than the second billing period following the second notice 
of violation, to the appropriate firm service classification unless (i) the Company has been notified in 
writing that the Customer has chosen to terminate gas service or (ii) the Customer has been notified that 
the Company has determined, in its sole discretion that it cannot provide firm service to the Customer and 
service would be terminated in thirty (30) days. If a customer fails to switch to its alternate fuel during a 
TC interruption that occurs within 48 hours of a previous interruption, the customer will not earn a second 
strike. 
   
8. Customers are responsible for the maintenance and repair of their dual-fuel equipment and all associated 
non-National Grid controls. You are required to manually switch to your alternate fuel if any control device 
fails to switch the equipment over to the alternate fuel when the outside temperature falls below the 
designated temperature setting. Please notify the Company at 1-718-643-4050 if you experience any 
equipment difficulty or alternate fuel issues. 
  
9. Customers must have qualified personnel available to curtail the use of natural gas 24 hours a day, 7 days a 
week. 
 
If you have any questions regarding your TC service or require a copy of the Company’s TC tariff, please contact 
your account executive or Account Services at 1-718-270-5990. 
 
Sincerely, 
 
National Grid 
 
 
 
 
Exhibit__(GL-2)

Dear Customer: 
 
The purpose of this letter is to notify you that on November 14, 2017 National Grid (“Company”) will perform a

alarm to switch over from gas until such time they receive an alarm notifying them that they can switch

adequate alternate fuel on hand to sustain this interruption. If you have any equipment issues, it is 
imperative that you contact the Company within one (1) hour of when you or your operating personnel 
become aware of any equipment issues.

from switching to an alternate fuel.

switch to its alternate fuel during a TC interruption that occurs within 48 hours of a previous 
interruption, the customer will not earn a second strike. 
 
8. Customers are responsible for the maintenance and repair of their dual-fuel equipment and all associated 
non-National Grid controls. You are required to manually switch to your alternate fuel if any control device 
fails to switch the equipment over to the alternate fuel when the outside temperature falls below the 
designated temperature setting. Please notify the Company at 1-800-930-5003 if you experience any 
equipment difficulty or alternate fuel issues.  
 
9. Customers must have qualified personnel available to curtail the use of natural gas 24 hours a day, 7 
days a week. 
 
If you have any questions regarding your TC service or require a copy of the Company’s TC tariff, please contact 
your account executive or Account Services at 1-718-270-5990. 
 
Sincerely, 
 
National Grid

Dear Customer: 
 
This purpose of this letter is to notify you that on November 14, 2017 National Grid (“National Grid” or “Company”) 
will perform a test to confirm your ability to comply with the interruption provisions of the Company’s interruptible 
gas tariff, specifically the tariff’s requirement that interruptible customers maintain alternate fuel capabilities that 
will be available in the event of a system interruption. Please take the time to share this information with all 
individuals responsible for operating and maintaining the heating equipment at your premises. Please refer to the 
tariff for additional provisions.  
 
1. National Grid will attempt to notify you of the upcoming test or an interruption by calling the telephone 
number that is currently on file for your account. If this number is no longer valid, please call us at           
1-800-930-5003 so that we can update our records with the correct number. 
 
2. There is a test scheduled for November 14, 2017 beginning at 8:00 am. National Grid will call you 
instructing you to switch over your dual-fuel equipment for up to eight (8) hours. If you have any 
equipment issues, it is imperative that you contact the Company within one hour of when you or your 
operating personnel become aware of the equipment issues. 
 
3. Please promptly notify National Grid at 1-800-930-5003 of any condition that may prevent your equipment 
from switching to an alternate fuel. 
 
 
Tariff Provisions: National Grid would like to remind you of the tariff provisions for Interruptible (“IT”) service 
under either Service Classification No. 4 (Sales) or Service Classification No. 7 (Transportation). 
 
1. If at any time, the Company needs to call on its peaking supplies to serve firm customers, or if the 
Company, in its sole discretion, determines that it is necessary to protect the reliability and safety of the 
system for firm customers, the Company may interrupt service to all customers served under the 
interruptible service classes. The Company will endeavor to provide notification (within two hours) of this 
scheduled interruption. A customer that continues to take gas after receiving notice of interruption by the 
Company may incur additional charges as provided in the interruptible tariff. 
 
2. Your dual-fuel equipment and controls must be in good working order. In addition, you must have a 
minimum ten (10) day supply of alternate fuel on hand. The “on hand” requirement can be satisfied by 
either storing the full ten-day fuel requirement onsite, or through a combination of onsite storage and a 
delivery contract with a fuel supplier for the difference. Whenever National Grid becomes aware that a 
Customer’s equipment is incapable of switching over to alternate fuel and/or that the customer does not 
have an adequate supply of alternate fuel on hand, the Company shall notify the Customer that the 
violation must be corrected within ten (10) calendar days. It shall be the responsibility of the Customer to 
contact the Company and provide proof that the violation has been corrected. With regard to an 
inadequate fuel situation and/or equipment failure, customers who have not supplied the Company with 
such proof shall be subject to a variable rate (non-compliance charge) that is higher than the monthly 
posted rate as provided for in the Company’s IT tariff. The non-compliance charge will cease the day the 
Company receives satisfactory proof that the violation has been corrected. 
 
3. National Grid must be permitted to install, access, inspect and repair remote meter reading and fuel 
switching devices and associated controls located on your premises as required to assure proper 
operation and monitoring of your system. It is the Customer’s responsibility to avoid damage to or 
disconnection of the Company’s equipment. Any such event must be reported to the Company 
immediately. 
 
4. During each winter season, any Customer who does not interrupt gas usage when required to do so, 
including during any system-wide tests, will be notified that they have violated the requirements of the 
tariff. Please provide National Grid representatives with prompt access when they come to inspect your 
equipment. Although they may arrive at any time and without prior notice, the Company will always try to 
minimize any inconvenience this may cause. 
 
Exhibit__(GL-2)

5. Any failure to interrupt gas usage when required to do so will result in penalties and potentially changes in 
service. Any gas consumed (above the minimum tolerance level established in the tariff) when there is an 
interruption will result in unauthorized use charges as provided for in the Company’s IT tariff. In addition, if 
proof is not provided within ten calendar (10) days that the violation has been remedied, your account will 
also incur a non-compliance charge as mentioned in item (2) above. If a customer fails to switch to its 
alternate fuel during an interruption that occurs within 48 hours of a previous interruption, the customer 
will not earn a second strike. 
 
6. Customers who receive two violations within one winter season (November through March) will have their 
account transferred, effective with their next billing period following the second notice of violation, to the 
appropriate firm service classification, unless (i) the Company has been notified in writing that the 
Customer has chosen to terminate gas service or (ii) the Customer has been notified that the Company 
has determined in its sole discretion that it cannot provide firm service to the Customer and service would 
be terminated in thirty (30) days. 
 
7. Customers are responsible for the maintenance and repair of their dual-fuel equipment and all associated 
non-National Grid controls. 
 
8. Customers must have qualified personnel available to cease the use of natural gas 24 hours a day, 7 
days a week. 
 
9. National Grid has a communications system that will enable you to receive information regarding your 
account. This system is intended to enhance real-time communications with interruptible customers 
regarding their natural gas service, including notifications regarding service interruptions. In addition, to 
receiving traditional automated telephone calls, this system will allow you to receive messages via 
different forms of communications, including emails, texts and facsimile. You can register by visiting 
https://ngrid.myenergysites.com/nonfirm-communication. Please take a few moments to register on the 
website and fill out the requested information. If you have any questions, please feel free to contact your 
Account Manager or contact us at 718-270-5990. 
If you have any questions, regarding your Interruptible Service or require a copy of the Company’s IT tariff, 
please contact your account executive or Account Services at 718-270-5990.
